Heart as the seat of self-judgment (conscience function in the OT): "David's heart struck him" (1Sa 24:5; 2Sa 24:10); "pangs of conscience" — ESV for *lēv* (1Sa 25:31); "Your heart knows that many times you yourself have cursed others" (Ecc 7:22); "my heart does not reproach me" (Job 27:6); NT: "whenever our heart condemns us, God is greater than our heart … if our heart does not condemn us, we have confidence" (1Jo 3:20–21)

##### Observation 12187: stage strand-reading, tag key-statement, status draft, deleted 0

- basis: stated; run_id: CATCHUP-2172-B-20261022-anger; created_at: 2026-10-22T11:16:10Z
- **obs_text:** Strife: where it comes from, what holds it, what ends it.
- **setting:** Abram and Lot's herdsmen (Gen 13:7–9); the Proverbs.
- **meaning:** the verses name a source for strife (a perverted heart, a quarrelsome man, wine, a backbiting tongue), what it is like once in place (bars of a castle, an offended brother), and what ends it (the lot; Abram giving Lot the choice of the land; "where there is no whisperer, quarreling ceases", Pro 26:20).
- **implication:** strife is spoken of as kindled, like fire (Pro 26:20–21), in the heat picture of unit 2. Gen 13:8, Pro 18:19 are in 10.10.
- **chain:** perverted heart → sowing discord (Pro 6:14) · quarrelsome man → kindling strife (26:21) · no whisperer → quarreling ceases (26:20) · tarry over wine → strife, wounds (23:29–30) · backbiting tongue → angry looks (25:23) · no strife, for we are kinsmen → choose the land (Gen 13:8–9)
- **parties:** Abram, Lot and the herdsmen; the worthless man; the quarrelsome man; brothers; the drinker; the backbiter
- **revisit_note:** October disposition: D (Gen 13:8; Pro 18:19); W; also appears: 10.10; 10.5 (_analytics/Clusters/M02 - anger-wrath/anger-observation-ledger-unit4-v1-20261005.md)

##### Strong's entry G2588 καρδία kardia "heart"

- language Greek; native word καρδία; transliteration kardia; STEP gloss "heart"; sense head "heart" (the code is its own lemma); origin word

###### Meaning trees stored under the lemma G2588 (1, strong_meaning_tree; the key is the lemma, so the trees of its variants are stored together and shown in stored order, a repeated tree as stored)

**Tree 1** (rows 5266 to 5266)

- <b>the heart,</b> regarded as the seat of feeling, impulse, affection, desire, <ref='Mat.6.21; 22:37'>Mt. 6:21; 22:37</ref>; <ref='Php.1.7'>Phil. 1:7;</ref> <b>the heart, mind</b> (seat of thought and emotion). The heart was thought to be the seat of the inner self (composed of life, soul, mind, and spirit). "Heart" is similar in meaning to "soul," but often the "heart" has a focus on thinking and understanding (<ref='Mrk.2.8'>Mk 2:8</ref>; <ref='Luk.1.51; 24:38'>Lk 1:51; 24:38</ref>); <b>the heart,</b> regarded as the seat of feeling, impulse, affection, desire, <ref='Mat.6.21; 22:37'>Mt. 6:21; 22:37</ref>; <ref='Php.1.7'>Phil. 1:7;</ref> <b>the heart,</b> as the seat of intellect, <ref='Mat.13.15'>Mt. 13:15</ref>; <ref='Rom.1.21'>Rom. 1:21;</ref> <b>the heart,</b> as the inner and mental frame, <ref='Mat.5.8'>Mt. 5:8</ref>; <ref='Luk.16.15'>Lk. 16:15</ref>; <ref='1Pe.3.4'>1Pet. 3:4;</ref> <b>the conscience,</b> <ref='1Jn.3.20, 21'>1Jn. 3:20, 21</ref>; <b>the heart, the inner part, middle, center,</b> <ref='Mat.12.40'>Mt. 12:40</ref>

| I.2 | that | when ὅτι introduces a conditional sentence, the Constr. after ὅτι is the same as in independent conditional sentences, εἴ τις ἔροιτο, καθ᾽ ὁποίους νόμους δεῖ πολιτεύεσθαι, δῆλον ὅτι ἀποκρίναισθ᾽ ἄν.. it is manifest you would answer.., | lookup |
| II |  | ὅτι is frequently inserted pleonastic in introducing a quotation (where we use no conjunction and put inverted commas), λόγον τόνδε ἐκφαίνει ὁ Πρωτεύς, λέγων ὅτι ἐγὼ εἰ μὴ περὶ πολλοῦ ἡγεύμην.. ; καὶ ἐγὼ εἶπον, ὅ. ἡ αὐτή μοι ἀρχή ἐστι.. ; even where the quotation consists of one word, | lookup |
| II.2 |  | ὅ. is also used pleonastic with the infinitive and accusative ; but ὅτι has frequently been wrongly inserted by the copyists, as if εἶπεν or λέγουσιν must be followed by it, as in | lookup |
| III |  | ὅτι in Attic dialect frequently represents a whole sentence, especially in affirmative answers, οὐκοῦν.. τὸ ἀδικεῖν κάκιον ἂν εἴη τοῦ ἀδικεῖσθαι. Answ. δῆλον δὴ ὅτι (i.e. ὅτι κάκιον ἂν εἴη, or ὅτι ταῦτα οὕτως ἔχει) ; compare οἶδ᾽ ὅτι, ἴσθ᾽ ὅτι, οἶσθ᾽ ὅτι, adverb | lookup |
| III.2 |  | what we make the subject of the Verb which follows ὅτι frequently stands in the preceding clause, Αυκάονας δὲ καὶ αὐτοὶ εἴδομεν, ὅτι.. καρποῦνται (for εἴδομεν, ὅτι Λυκάονες καρποῦνται) | lookup |
| IV | with regard to the fact that | ὅτι sometimes = , ὅτι.. οὔ φησι.. ὄνομα εἶναι, ὑποπτεύω αὐτὸν σκώπτειν | lookup |
| V | not only, although | οὐχ ὅ..., ἀλλὰ or ἀλλὰ καὶ.., οὐχ ὅ. ὁ Κρίτων ἐν ἡσυχίᾳ ἦν, ἀλλὰ οἱ φίλοι αὐτοῦ .., but his friends, the powers in Europe, but.., ., not followed by a second clause, means , οὐχ ὅ. παίζει καί φησι | lookup |
| V.2 |  | for ὅτι μή, see at {ὅ τι} 11. | lookup |
| B | for that, because | as a causal Particle, , generally after Verbs of feeling, : but without such a Verb, ὃν περὶ πάσης τῖεν ὁμηλικίης, ὅτι οἱ φρεσὶν ἄρτια ᾔδη | lookup |
| B.b | why?, because why? | followed by τί, ὅτι τ; (literal ) ; ὅτι τί δ; ; ὅτι δὴ τί μάλιστ; ; ὅτι δὴ τί γ; ; compare ὁτιή. | lookup |
| B.2 | seeing that, as is proved by the fact that | , in giving the reason for saying what is said, γλαυκὴ δέ σε τίκτε θάλασσα.. ὅτι τοι νόος ἐστὶν ἀπηνής .., (ὅ τ᾽) probably always represents ὅτε (ὅ τε): there are no examples of ὅττ᾽: hiatus after ὅτι is permitted in Comedy texts, | lookup |

- **obs_text:** Points that follow from the scan, with no reading done: ʿānî (H6041) is the partner of ʿānāw and of "poor and needy". It shares 32 verses with M09. This accounts for much of M24's place as M09's largest co-occurring cluster; "Humility" as a noun in the OT (Pro 15:33; 18:12; 22:4, H6038) sits in M05, not M09; "Meek" and "gentle" in the NT (Mat 5:5; 11:29; Gal 5:23) sit in M05; H8213 šāphēl, "to bring low", sits in M53. Its 29 verses include 1Sa 2:7, Psa 18:27, Pro 29:23, Isa 2:9–17 and Eze 21:26.

(1Co 4:21) [X]; "you who are spiritual should restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Keep watch on yourself" (Gal 6:1) [X]; "the hidden person of the heart with the imperishable beauty of a gentle and quiet spirit" (1Pe 3:4) [H1]; "I am gentle and lowly in heart" (Mat 11:29); "receive with meekness the implanted word, which is able to save your souls" (Jam 1:21); "make a defense … with gentleness and respect, having a good conscience" (1Pe 3:15–16); [Claude reading] "Spirit" here names the manner in which one person comes to another (1Co 4:21; Gal 6:1) — the disposition [X] governing an encounter. Where gentleness is located in the heart (Mat 11:29), it is a settled character. Where it is "of spirit", it is the bearing brought to a specific meeting.

##### Observation 16988: stage prior-capture, tag statement, status draft, deleted 0

- basis: stated; run_id: RUN-20261026_153534_144-PRIORCAPTURE; created_at: 2026-10-26T15:36:52Z
- **obs_text:** 14. Cross-cutting observations for Batch F [Claude reading]: Relating to others is most often a matter of the soul.; Souls are knit (1Sa 18:1).; A soul loves, seeks and fails (Song 3; 5).; Malice lives in the soul (Eze 25:6, 15).; Souls abhor each other, even God's soul and the people's (Lev 26).; One loves another "as one's own soul".; The heart is where hatred starts and is spoken (Lev 19:17; Gen 27:41). It is also where the response to another's fall is judged (Pro 24:17).; The human spirit registers bonds and bearing.; It is unsettled by a friend's absence and refreshed by a friend's arrival (2Co 2:13; 7:13; 1Co 16:18).; It is the manner of approach — gentleness (1Co 4:21).; It is what the wise hold back and the fool vents (Pro 29:11).; Sequences matter more than single verses. Reading before and after changed the picture in six places: 1Sa 18 (Jonathan and Saul); Lev 26 (reciprocal abhorrence broken by God); Psa 143 and Psa 51 (the crushing chains); Judg 19 and the other merry-heart narratives (threshold states); 2Co 2 → 7 (the spirit's rest through persons); 2Ki 2 (spirit transfer confirmed by others); Meaning shifts with circumstance in consistent ways: *crush*: by whom, and turned to whom (§5); *tender heart*: to what it yields (§6); *good heart*: from God or from wine (§7); *love / hate one's soul*: well-being vs clinging (§3); *hāgâ*: what the heart rehearses (§12.2); *nûaḥ*: what rests in or on the person (§9.5); Inner states spread between people: the fainthearted soldier melts others (Deu 20:8); heart-speech is reported (Gen 27:42); a lowly heart gives rest to other souls (Mat 11:29); a congregation refreshes Titus's spirit (2Co 7:13); the prophet's heart takes in the people's doom (Jer 4:18–19); Other spirits relate to people as occupants of a house (Mat 12:43–45) and as seizures between people (1Sa 18:10; Judg 9:23 in Batch D). The divine Spirit rests upon people and is recognised by others (Num 11; 2Ki 2).; Fringe worth keeping: the knit soul beside the seizing spirit in one chapter (1Sa 18); the heart awake while the body lags (Song 5); God refusing to complete reciprocal abhorrence (Lev 26:44); Jezebel curing a vexed spirit by a manufactured good heart (1Ki 21); the prophet's heart for Moab set against Ammon's malice of soul (Isa 15; Jer 48; Eze 25); past hatred as a legal criterion (Deu 19)
- **revisit_note:** Captured from _analytics/Clusters/M47 - inner-seat/M47-x-relating-to-others-batchF-v4-20260928.md lines 600-638 (#2305)

##### Section 370: chapter 10.14 Humility, Jesus

> ## Jesus
> 
> In the New Testament, humility rests on what the verses say of Jesus. God's own part is in 13.1, *Lowering himself*. Here is what it means for the one who humbles himself.
> 
> **He is lowly.** "learn from me, for I am gentle and lowly in heart, and you will find rest for your souls" (Matthew 11:29). His own word on his own heart. Rest is found by learning from it.
> 
> **He came humble.** "your king is coming to you, humble, and mounted on a donkey" (Matthew 21:5; Zechariah 9:9). As a boy who knew "I must be in my Father's house", "he went down with them and came to Nazareth and was submissive to them" (Luke 2:49, 51). "In his humiliation justice was denied him", and like a lamb he "opens not his mouth" (Acts 8:32–33).
> 
> **He humbled himself, and was exalted.** "who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross. Therefore God has highly exalted him" (Philippians 2:6–9). "we see him who for a little while was made lower than the angels, namely Jesus, crowned with glory and honor because of the suffering of death" (Hebrews 2:9). I read the saying he taught, "whoever humbles himself will be exalted", as what is told of him.
> 
> **He is the ground of humility toward others.**
> - "Have this mind among yourselves, which is yours in Christ Jesus" (Philippians 2:5) is the reason for "in humility count others more significant than yourselves" (2:3).
> - "leaving you an example, so that you might follow in his steps" (1 Peter 2:21).
> - "out of reverence for Christ" (Ephesians 5:21).
> - Paul appeals "by the meekness and gentleness of Christ" (2 Corinthians 10:1).
> 
> **Others lower themselves before him.** John the Baptist: "He must increase, but I must decrease" (John 3:30). He says it after "I am not the Christ", and with "this joy of mine is now complete" (3:28–29). His lowering is said with joy.
> 
> **At his coming, a low estate is looked on.** Mary: "he has looked on the humble estate of his servant" (Luke 1:48). Her song goes on as Hannah's had: he scatters "the proud in the thoughts of their hearts", brings down the mighty and exalts the humble, fills the hungry and sends the rich away (Luke 1:51–53; 1 Samuel 2:7–8).

##### Section 371: chapter 10.14 Humility, The Old Testament and the New

> ## The Old Testament and the New
> 
> Everything above is in both Testaments:
> - humbling oneself before God, and God's answer
> - the proud brought low and the humble raised
> - favour to the humble
> - the lower seat
> - the meek inheriting
> - God humbling for good
> - a humbling he does not accept
> - humility beside hearing
> - the humble king
> - God lowering himself
> 
> The New Testament quotes the Old on three of them (Proverbs 3:34; Zechariah 9:9; Psalm 8:5–6).
> 
> What differs is emphasis, and one thing that is new.
> 
> - **Before God, then also toward one another.** In the Old Testament humbling is almost always before God. Kings and the people humble themselves, and the answer is wrath turned or disaster delayed. In the New, much of what is said is about humility toward one another. It always has a reason that faces God, and the answer is named as being justified, grace, and rest for the soul.
> - **The new thing: Christ.** He is the one who is lowly in heart, who humbled himself to death, and in whose mind the humble live. What God did in looking down, in his gentleness and in his breaking over a heart, the Son did in a human life. What the Old Testament said of man, "you have made him a little lower than the heavenly beings … you have put all things under his feet" (Psalm 8:5–6), the letter to the Hebrews reads as "namely Jesus" (Hebrews 2:6–9).

##### Section 380: chapter 13.1 Divine, Lowering himself

> ## Lowering himself
> 
> The Lord is high, and the verses keep saying that he looks low.
> - "Who is like the Lord our God, who is seated on high, who looks far down on the heavens and the earth? He raises the poor from the dust" (Psalm 113:5–7). The verb for looking far down is the one used when he brings the proud low.
> - "For though the Lord is high, he regards the lowly, but the haughty he knows from afar" (Psalm 138:6).
> - "I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit, to revive the spirit of the lowly, and to revive the heart of the contrite" (Isaiah 57:15).
> - David: "You have given me the shield of your salvation, and your gentleness made me great" (2 Samuel 22:36; Psalm 18:35). The word rendered "gentleness" is the word for humility.
> - Over his people's unfaithfulness: "how I have been broken over their whoring heart that has departed from me" (Ezekiel 6:9).
> 
> **In the Son.** "who, though he was in the form of God, did not count equality with God a thing to be grasped, but emptied himself, by taking the form of a servant, being born in the likeness of men. And being found in human form, he humbled himself by becoming obedient to the point of death, even death on a cross. Therefore God has highly exalted him" (Philippians 2:6–9). He says of himself, "I am gentle and lowly in heart" (Matthew 11:29). He came "humble, and mounted on a donkey" (Matthew 21:5). As a boy, "he went down with them and came to Nazareth and was submissive to them" (Luke 2:51). "In his humiliation justice was denied him" (Acts 8:33). He "for a little while was made lower than the angels … crowned with glory and honor because of the suffering of death" (Hebrews 2:9). These are the words of Psalm 8 about man, read of Jesus (Psalm 8:5). All things are put under him, and at the end "the Son himself will also be subjected to him who put all things in subjection under him, that God may be all in all" (1 Corinthians 15:28).
> 
> I read the lowering that the Old Testament tells of God, looking down, dwelling with the lowly, his gentleness, his being broken over a heart, as done in the Son in a human life.
> 
> **Toward the humble and the proud.** He sees the one who humbles himself and answers: "When the Lord saw that they humbled themselves" (2 Chronicles 12:7); "Have you seen how Ahab has humbled himself before me?" (1 Kings 21:29). He gives favour: "to the humble he gives favor" (Proverbs 3:34); "God opposes the proud but gives grace to the humble" (James 4:6). He humbles to test, "to do you good in the end" (Deuteronomy 8:16). He brings the proud low, "and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11). He looks on a humble estate (Luke 1:48). What this means for the one who humbles himself is in 10.14.
